The abstractions you're designing are fundamental patterns for how Chemicals relate to each other.
Let me think about this properly:
typescript// Association maps members to relationships
class $Association<TMember extends $Chemical, TRelationship> extends $Chemical {
    members: Map<TRelationship, TMember> = new Map();
    
    associate(relationship: TRelationship, member: TMember) {
        this.members.set(relationship, member);
        member.parent = this;
    }
    
    get(relationship: TRelationship): TMember | undefined {
        return this.members.get(relationship);
    }
    
    view() {
        // Render current association based on some selection logic
        return this.children;
    }
}

// Container holds a collection of elements
class $Container<TElement extends $Chemical> extends $Chemical {
    elements: TElement[] = [];
    
    $Container(...elements: TElement[]) {
        this.elements = elements;
        elements.forEach(el => el.parent = this);
    }
    
    view() {
        return (
            <>
                {this.elements.map(el => <el.Component key={el.key} />)}
            </>
        );
    }
}
These are Chemistry-level abstractions. A router built ON TOP of Chemistry would use $Association to map paths to pages. But $Association itself is about the general pattern of relating Chemicals through some key/relationship.
This is like how React has concepts like "children" and "context" but doesn't have a router built in. Chemistry would provide the association/container patterns, and router libraries would use them.
Is that closer to what you're envisioning? The fundamental patterns for relating and organizing Chemicals?RetryDYes, but I am wondering how the router might work. How do they work? If I want pages to be separate, then I need a router that, what - if it's a static app, it just fetches the page from there. If it was fancy it might use cookies to help reconstruct stateIf it's a dynamic thing, then what I use the browser API to change routes, but it's not a real page because if refreshed the app might not respond the same, or we would have to setup the abstraction where it wouldRouters fundamentally solve different problems based on the app type:
Static Site (GitHub Pages style)

Each route maps to an actual HTML file
Browser handles navigation naturally
Refresh works because /about loads about.html
State lives in localStorage/cookies if needed
Chemistry would just enhance each page after load

Single Page App (SPA)

One HTML file, JavaScript changes content
Router intercepts link clicks, prevents page reload
Uses History API to change URL without reload
Refresh breaks unless server configured to always serve index.html
State lives in memory (lost on refresh unless persisted)

Server-Side Rendered (SSR)

Server generates HTML for each route
Hydrates to SPA after initial load
Refresh works because server handles the route
State can be serialized into HTML

For Chemistry with $Association, you'd probably want the SPA pattern:
typescriptclass $Router extends $Association<$Page, string> {
    constructor() {
        super();
        // Intercept browser navigation
        window.addEventListener('popstate', this.handleRoute);
    }
    
    handleRoute = () => {
        const path = window.location.pathname;
        const page = this.get(path);
        if (page) {
            // Chemistry handles the re-render
            this.currentPage = page;
        }
    }
    
    navigate(path: string) {
        window.history.pushState(null, '', path);
        this.handleRoute();
    }
}
The refresh problem is real - in a pure SPA, refreshing /about would 404 unless:

Server redirects all routes to index.html
You use hash routing (/#/about)
You pre-render each route as static HTML

State persistence across refresh would need explicit serialization to localStorage or cookies, then reconstruction on load. That's where your $App.start() method would come in - rebuilding the object graph from persisted state.RetryD"State lives in localStorage/cookies if needed" - what other options are there besides cookies on the web? I actually thing I want the whole-page pattern if the first usage of this is github pages. They are indexed better as pages no?For client-side storage on the web, you have:

localStorage (5-10MB, persistent)
sessionStorage (same as localStorage but expires on tab close)
IndexedDB (much larger, for complex data)
WebSQL (deprecated)
Cookies (small, sent with every request)

For GitHub Pages with better SEO, you're right - you want actual HTML files for each route. This is the static site generation approach:
typescript// Build time - generate static files
class $StaticRouter extends $Association<$Page, string> {
    async generateStaticSite() {
        for (const [path, PageClass] of this.members) {
            const page = new PageClass();
            const html = ReactDOMServer.renderToString(page.Component);
            
            // Write to /public/[path]/index.html
            // /about -> /about/index.html
            // GitHub Pages serves it as /about/
        }
    }
}
Each page would be:

Its own HTML file with full content
SEO-friendly (crawlers see the content)
Fast initial load (no JS required to show content)
Chemistry enhances it after load (hydration)

For state persistence between pages in this model:

URL parameters for visible state (/search?q=chemistry)
localStorage for user preferences
Cookies for auth tokens

The challenge is sharing Chemistry instances between pages. Each page load creates new instances. You'd need to serialize/deserialize the object graph, or treat each page as independent with shared localStorage for continuity.
This is why frameworks like Next.js exist - they give you static generation for SEO plus SPA behavior after the first load. But for pure GitHub Pages, separate HTML files with Chemistry enhancement is probably the cleanest approach.RetryDCan I have things triggered to clicking a link that goes to a new page? localStorage/sessionStorage
Simple key-value stores for strings:
typescript// Before navigating to new page
document.addEventListener('click', (e) => {
    if (e.target.tagName === 'A') {

            currentUser: 'John',

    const state = JSON.parse(saved);
    // Reconstruct Chemistry objects from state
}
Limitations:

Strings only (must JSON.stringify objects)
Synchronous (can block UI if storing large data)
~5-10MB limit
Can't store functions, class instances directly

IndexedDB
Database-like storage for complex data:
typescript// Open database
const db = await new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const request = indexedDB.open('ChemistryDB', 1);
    request.onsuccess = () => resolve(request.result);
    request.onerror = () => reject(request.error);
});

// Store complex data
const tx = db.transaction(['chemicals'], 'readwrite');
const store = tx.objectStore('chemicals');
store.put({
    id: 'dictionary-1',
    entries: [...],  // Can store arrays, objects, even Blobs
    timestamp: Date.now()
});

// Retrieve
const request = store.get('dictionary-1');
request.onsuccess = () => {
    const data = request.result;
    // Reconstruct Chemistry objects
};
Serializing Chemistry Objects
Chemistry objects can't be stored directly (methods and symbols are lost). You'd need:
typescriptclass $Chemical {
    // Serialize to plain object
    serialize() {
        const data: any = {};
        for (const key in this) {
            if (!key.startsWith('$') && typeof this[key] !== 'function') {
                data[key] = this[key];
            }
        }
        data.__type = this.constructor.name;
        return data;
    }
    
    // Reconstruct from plain object
    static deserialize(data: any): $Chemical {
        const TypeClass = chemicalRegistry.get(data.__type);
        const instance = new TypeClass();
        Object.assign(instance, data);
        return instance;
    }
}
For GitHub Pages with full page navigation, you'd typically:

Serialize state on link click
Store in localStorage
New page loads, reads localStorage
Reconstructs Chemistry objects
Clears or updates stored state

This gives you persistence across page loads while keeping actual separate HTML pages for SEO.RetryDWhat if I had the abstraction of the atom, where somehow it's an auto-singletop. Atoms are cached forever - one per class. So that if an atom is ever created, it is guaranteed to be the same one somehow - even if different instances, all share the same stateAnd then in the atom, there is an abstraction to bind its representation to something like local storageAn atom singleton pattern with automatic persistence - that's a clever approach for cross-page state in Chemistry:
typescriptclass $Atom extends $Chemical {
    private static instances = new Map<string, $Atom>();
    private static storageKey = 'chemistry-atoms';
    
    constructor() {
        super();
        const className = this.constructor.name;
        
        // Return existing instance if it exists
        if ($Atom.instances.has(className)) {
            return $Atom.instances.get(className)!;
        }
        
        // Create new instance and store it
        $Atom.instances.set(className, this);
        this.hydrate();
        
        // Auto-persist on any change
        // But how to detect changes without proxies everywhere?
    }
    
    private hydrate() {
        const stored = localStorage.getItem($Atom.storageKey);
        if (stored) {
            const atoms = JSON.parse(stored);
            const className = this.constructor.name;
            if (atoms[className]) {
                Object.assign(this, atoms[className]);
            }
        }
    }
    
    persist() {
        const atoms: Record<string, any> = {};
        for (const [name, instance] of $Atom.instances) {
            atoms[name] = instance.serialize();
        }
        localStorage.setItem($Atom.storageKey, JSON.stringify(atoms));
    }
}

// Usage across pages
class $UserAtom extends $Atom {
    username = '';
    theme = 'light';
}

// Page 1
const user = new $UserAtom();  // Creates or retrieves singleton
user.username = 'John';
user.persist();
// Navigate to page 2...

// Page 2 
const user = new $UserAtom();  // Gets same data from localStorage
console.log(user.username);  // 'John'

Best to learn how to be helpful, not to just blindly try and do2 / 2The Atom pattern raises some interesting architectural challenges for Chemistry. Let me think through what you're describing:
The core idea seems to be that Atoms are singleton-like, but not in the traditional sense. Every new $UserAtom() returns a different object reference, but they all share the same underlying state - they're all bound to the template/prototype. This is different from Chemicals where each instance has its own state.
For persistence, if all instances share state, you only need to persist once. But the tricky part is knowing when state changes. Since Atoms would bypass the normal bond system (no props), you'd need a different mechanism to detect changes and trigger persistence.
The rerender problem is particularly interesting. If an Atom is used as a property in multiple Chemicals:
typescriptclass $Header extends $Chemical {
    user = new $UserAtom();
}

class $Sidebar extends $Chemical {  
    user = new $UserAtom();
}
When the Atom's state changes, both Header and Sidebar need to rerender. But the Atom doesn't know who's using it - there's no parent relationship like with normal Chemicals. You'd need some kind of observer pattern where Chemicals register themselves when they access an Atom.
The "protected constructor" idea makes sense to enforce the pattern, but you still want people to call new $UserAtom() naturally. Maybe the constructor returns a proxy that routes to the shared state?
For props, allowing them but having them update all instances globally is conceptually odd but could be powerful. It's like broadcast props - set it once, affects everywhere.
What's the use case driving this? Is it primarily for auth/user state that needs to persist across pages and be globally accessible?RetryDSee this is what I mean. No props doesn't mean no state changes. All properties in chemistry that don't have underlines or are captialized are reactive. I am just saying that if one writes:<Atom x={5} /> the 5 won't make a difference. And when the formula is created, perhaps an atom will throw an error if it has a prop-like propertyThat makes it reactive, just not externally configurable. typescriptclass $UserAtom extends $Atom {

The formula would need to know it's dealing with an Atom and skip the prop binding phase. Any $property on an Atom would throw an error since props make no sense when all instances share state.
For the shared state mechanism, if all instances are bound to the template, you'd need to modify how Chemistry creates instances. Instead of each instance having its own bonds, Atoms would have their bonds on the prototype/template itself. When any instance changes a property, it's actually changing the template's property, which affects all instances.
The tricky part is the rerender triggering. When an Atom's property changes:

The change happens on the template
All Chemicals that have an instance of that Atom need to know
But Atoms don't have parents, they're more like... ambient state

You'd almost need a subscription system where Chemicals register which Atoms they use during their render, then get notified when those Atoms change. This is different from the parent-child reactivity Chemistry normally uses.
The persistence could hook into the same change detection - whenever an Atom property changes, serialize the template's state to localStorage. On app load, hydrate all Atom templates before any components render.

typescriptif (this instanceof $Atom) {

The shared state mechanism is the interesting implementation detail. When you create a new instance of an Atom, it needs to:

Check if a template exists
If yes, bind to that template's state
If no, become the template

The bonds would exist on the template, not the instances. When you do atom.username = 'John', it's actually updating the template's bond, which all instances share.
For persistence, whenever any Atom template's state changes, it could serialize itself. On app startup, before any components render, Chemistry would hydrate all Atom templates from localStorage.
The challenge is making this work with Chemistry's existing formula/bond system without breaking the assumptions it makes about instance independence. The Atom would need its own formula type or a flag that changes how bonds are created and managed.RetryDI think the template will be reactivated and atoms will have the special property that all methods wrap the templateSo the template becomes the single source of truth for all Atom instances. When you create a new Atom instance, its methods are essentially proxies that operate on the template's state:
typescriptclass $Atom extends $Chemical {
    constructor() {
        super();
        const template = this.constructor.prototype;
        
        // Reactivate the template if needed
        if (!template[$formula]) {
            template[$formula] = new $Formula(template);
            template[$formula].init();
        }
        
        // All methods on this instance actually operate on the template
        for (const key of Object.getOwnPropertyNames(this)) {
            if (typeof this[key] === 'function' && key !== 'constructor') {
                const originalMethod = this[key];
                this[key] = (...args) => {
                    // Switch context to template for execution
                    return originalMethod.apply(template, args);
                };
            }
        }
    }
}
This means when any instance calls a method or accesses a property, it's actually working with the template's state. The bonds exist on the template, the formula tracks changes on the template, and all instances are just lightweight references to that shared template.
The tricky part is making sure React components that use different instances of the same Atom all re-render when the template changes. Each instance's Component would need to subscribe to the template's formula somehow.
For persistence, the template could serialize its state on change, and on app load, Chemistry would need to hydrate all Atom templates before any components try to use them. This ensures the singleton behavior persists across page loads.RetryDNo, atoms can be made to have empty constructors, so that one can always be made. If the state is understood to be persisted, you are kind of constructing the atom by configuring it after it has been createdRight, Atoms have empty constructors because they're not configured through construction - they're configured by mutating the shared state after creation. This makes sense for persistence:
typescriptclass $UserAtom extends $Atom {
    username = '';
    theme = 'light';
    
    // Empty constructor - no parameters
    constructor() {
        super();
        // State is either hydrated from storage or these are the defaults
    }
    
    // Configure by mutation after creation
    login(username: string) {
        this.username = username;  // Actually updates template
        this.loggedIn = true;
    }
}

// Usage
const user = new $UserAtom();  // Always works, no params needed
user.login('John');  // Configure through methods
The pattern becomes:

Create an Atom with new (always succeeds, no params)
It either hydrates existing state or uses defaults
Configure it through property assignment or methods
All instances see the changes

This is different from Chemicals where the constructor parameters matter for DI. Atoms don't participate in constructor-based DI because they have no constructor parameters. They're ambient state that you grab when needed.
The implementation challenge is making all property access and method calls redirect to the template while still having each instance be a distinct object (so they can have different parents in the object graph). Each instance would be like a proxy to the shared template, but still participate as a normal Chemical in terms of parent-child relationships.RetryDBut upon creation, if an atom finds that it is the template, it uses its persistence function to get a version of itself, and then reactivate itself, and then all other instances will have a formula equal to the templates. Upon refresh, if a new property is added, it is added to the template, refreshed there and then decorated. I think that's a coherent systemYes, that's a coherent system. The first Atom instance to be created becomes (or finds it is) the template and takes responsibility for initialization:
typescriptclass $Atom extends $Chemical {
    constructor() {
        super();
        const template = this.constructor.prototype;
        
        if (!template[$formula]) {
            // This is the first instance - I am the template
            this.hydrate(); // Load persisted state
            template[$formula] = new $Formula(template);
            template[$formula].init();
            // All properties now reactive on template
        } else {
            // Template exists - point to its formula
            this[$formula] = template[$formula];
        }
    }
    
    hydrate() {
        // Subclasses override to load from localStorage/etc
        const stored = this.loadFromStorage();
        if (stored) {
            Object.assign(this, stored);
        }
    }
}
The refresh case is interesting - if the class definition changes between page loads (new property added in development), the template would:

Load the persisted state (old properties)
Initialize with class defaults (new properties get default values)
Reactivate the formula with all properties
New properties become reactive alongside restored ones

This gives you both persistence and development flexibility. The system self-heals when the class definition evolves.
The formula sharing is clever - all instances point to the template's formula, so they all trigger the same reactivity. When any instance appears to change a property, it's actually changing the template, and all instances reflect that change.
For persistence triggers, the template's formula could auto-persist on any state change, making the whole thing transparent to users of the Atom.RetryDWhat happens with a protected constructor? Like, no one can call it right? But then it allows people to call the constructor except the class. If a constructor is made protected, is that viral?In TypeScript, protected constructors mean:

The class itself can call it
Subclasses can call it (via super())
External code cannot call it with new

For inheritance, it's not automatically viral:
typescriptclass Base {
    protected constructor() { }
}

class Child extends Base {
    // Can have public constructor
    constructor() {
        super();  // Can call protected parent constructor
    }
}

// new Base();  // Error!
// new Child(); // Works!
But for Atoms, you might not want this - if $Atom has a protected constructor, people couldn't create instances at all. You probably want:
typescriptclass $Atom extends $Chemical {
    // Public constructor with no params enforced
    constructor() {
        if (arguments.length > 0) {
            throw new Error('Atoms cannot have constructor parameters');
        }
        super();
        // ... template binding logic
    }
}
This allows new $UserAtom() but prevents new $UserAtom(someParam).
Alternatively, if you want to prevent direct instantiation but allow it through a factory:
typescriptclass $Atom {
    protected constructor() { }
    
    static create<T extends $Atom>(this: new() => T): T {
        // Special logic to bypass protected
        return new (this as any)();
    }
}

// const user = new $UserAtom();  // Error!
// const user = $UserAtom.create();  // Works!
But that adds complexity without much benefit for the Atom pattern where you want people to naturally write new $UserAtom().RetryDI see. Or do I want the semantics to be - initialize is only called if it can't find a representation from sync! HmmThe initialize-vs-sync semantics is an interesting design choice. Here are the two patterns you're considering:
Pattern 1: Initialize always, sync overlays
typescriptconstructor() {
    super();
    this.initialize();  // Sets defaults
    this.sync();        // Overlays persisted state on top
}
This means defaults are always set, then persistence overwrites them. Simple but potentially wasteful.
Pattern 2: Initialize only if no persisted state
typescriptconstructor() {
    super();
    if (!this.sync()) {  // sync returns false if nothing loaded
        this.initialize();  // Only set defaults if nothing persisted
    }
}
This is more efficient but requires sync to communicate whether it found anything.
The second pattern makes more sense for Atoms since they're singletons - you either restore the previous state OR initialize fresh, not both.
The challenge with calling sync in the constructor is that it needs to happen on the template (first instance), but you're in the middle of constructing that instance. You might need:
typescriptclass $Atom extends $Chemical {
    constructor() {
        super();
        const template = this.constructor.prototype;
        
        if (!template[$formula]) {
            // I am becoming the template
            if (!this.sync()) {
                this.initialize();
            }
            template[$formula] = new $Formula(template);
            template[$formula].init();
        } else {
            // Template exists, bind to it
            this[$formula] = template[$formula];
        }
    }
}
The initialize method gives subclasses a clean place to set defaults without touching the constructor, maintaining the parameterless constructor pattern while allowing configuration through a defined hook.RetryDOkay help me here:I want to use the constructor, assume it is parameterless, call it if particle is unassigned, and then call form:

typescript__getType<T extends $Type<$Chemical> = typeof $Chemical>(): T {

In a static method, this refers to the class constructor itself when called on a class. So:
typescriptclass $Atom extends $Chemical {
    private static _particle?: $Atom;
    
    static async particle<T extends $Atom>(this: typeof $Atom): Promise<T> {
        // 'this' is the constructor (e.g., $UserAtom when called as $UserAtom.particle())

        // Override for initialization
    }

You don't need this.prototype.__getType() in the static method because this is already the type/constructor. You'd use new this() to create an instance of whatever Atom subclass called particle().
The pattern ensures each Atom class has its own singleton instance stored in _particle.RetryDAre modules allowed to be async? Can you await something within one?No, JavaScript/TypeScript modules cannot be async at the top level in most environments. You cannot directly await in module scope:
typescript// This doesn't work in most environments:
const data = await fetch('/api/data');  // SyntaxError!
export const processedData = data.json();
However, there are a few patterns:
Top-level await (limited support)
Some environments (modern browsers, Node.js 14.8+) support top-level await in ES modules:
typescript// Only works in .mjs or with specific configuration
const data = await fetch('/api/data');
export default data;
Async initialization pattern
Most common approach:
typescript// atom.ts
class $UserAtom extends $Atom {
    static async init() {
        const instance = await this.particle();
        await instance.form();
        return instance;
    }
}

// Export the promise
export const UserAtom = $UserAtom.init();

// Consumer must await
import { UserAtom } from './atom';
const user = await UserAtom;  // Need to await
Export the class, let consumers initialize
typescript// atom.ts
export class $UserAtom extends $Atom { }
export const UserAtom = new $UserAtom().Component;  // Sync export

// Consumer handles async
const user = await $UserAtom.particle();
For Chemistry's Atom pattern, you probably want the third approach - export the class and Component synchronously, let consumers call particle() when they need the singleton with its async initialization.RetryDShould assume that reading and writing from thes storage mechanisms is async? I suppose I must in the general caseYes, you should assume storage operations are async in the general case:
localStorage/sessionStorage are actually synchronous:
typescript// These are sync
localStorage.setItem('key', 'value');
const value = localStorage.getItem('key');
IndexedDB is always async:
typescript// Always returns promises or uses callbacks
const db = await openDB();
const value = await db.get('key');
Future compatibility - Even if using localStorage now, you might want IndexedDB later for larger data.
For Chemistry's Atom persistence, making it async is wise:
typescriptabstract class $Atom extends $Chemical {
    protected async persist(): Promise<void> {
        // Subclasses override with their storage mechanism
        localStorage.setItem(this.key, JSON.stringify(this.serialize()));
    }
    
    protected async hydrate(): Promise<boolean> {
        // Returns true if found data
        const stored = localStorage.getItem(this.key);
        if (stored) {
            Object.assign(this, JSON.parse(stored));
            return true;
        }
        return false;
    }
}
This way:

You can switch storage mechanisms without changing the API
You can add network storage later
You can handle storage errors properly with try/catch
The abstraction is consistent even if the underlying operation is sync

The form() method being async makes sense - it can await hydrate() and any other initialization needs.RetryDHow do I do chained async?

                this._particle[$formed] = formed;
                if (!this._particle[$formed])
                    await this._particle.form();
            })For chained async operations, you have a few issues in your code. You're mixing then() with await incorrectly. Here are the correct patterns:
Option 1: Pure async/await
typescriptconst formed = await this._particle.reform();
this._particle[$formed] = formed;
if (!this._particle[$formed]) {
    await this._particle.form();
}
Option 2: Pure promises with then
typescriptthis._particle.reform()
    .then(formed => {
        this._particle[$formed] = formed;
        if (!this._particle[$formed]) {
            return this._particle.form();  // Return the promise to chain
        }
    });
Option 3: Async function inside then (if you need await)
typescriptthis._particle.reform()
    .then(async (formed) => {
        this._particle[$formed] = formed;
        if (!this._particle[$formed]) {
            await this._particle.form();
        }
    });
Your current code has await inside a non-async arrow function, which is a syntax error. If you're already in an async context, Option 1 (pure async/await) is cleanest. If you need promise chaining, either return promises to chain them (Option 2) or mark the callback as async (Option 3).RetryDCan I return something else from a constructor? Can I use the constructor to enforce a singleton?Yes, you can return something else from a constructor to enforce a singleton pattern in JavaScript/TypeScript:
typescriptclass $Atom extends $Chemical {
    private static instances = new Map<typeof $Atom, $Atom>();
    
    constructor() {
        super();
        const Class = this.constructor as typeof $Atom;
        
        // If instance exists, return it instead of 'this'
        if ($Atom.instances.has(Class)) {
            return $Atom.instances.get(Class)!;
        }
        
        // Store this instance
        $Atom.instances.set(Class, this);
        // 'this' is returned implicitly
    }
}

// Usage
const atom1 = new $UserAtom();
const atom2 = new $UserAtom();
console.log(atom1 === atom2); // true! Same instance
When a constructor returns an object (not a primitive), JavaScript uses that returned object instead of the newly created instance. This is how singleton enforcement works.
However, there's a TypeScript issue: TypeScript assumes new Class() always returns a new instance, so it won't know these are the same:
typescriptconst atom1 = new $UserAtom();
const atom2 = new $UserAtom();
// TypeScript thinks these are different instances
// But at runtime they're the same object
This can cause confusion. That's why many prefer factory methods for singletons:
typescriptclass $Atom {
    private static instances = new Map();
    protected constructor() { } // Prevent direct instantiation
    
    static instance<T extends $Atom>(this: new() => T): T {
        if (!this.instances.has(this)) {
            this.instances.set(this, new this());
        }
        return this.instances.get(this);
    }
}
But if you want the natural new syntax for Chemistry, returning from the constructor works at runtime even if TypeScript doesn't fully understand it.RetryDLike this?
protected constructor() {
        super();
        if (this !== this[$template])
            return this[$template];
    }Yes, that's a clean approach! Using the template as the singleton reference:
typescriptprotected constructor() {
    super();
    if (this !== this[$template])
        return this[$template];
}
This would make every new $Atom() return the template instance after the first one. The first instance becomes the template and all subsequent calls return it.
But there's a complexity - this[$template] is set in the base $Chemical constructor to this:
typescript// In $Chemical constructor
this[$template] = this;
So you'd need to handle the case where it's the first instance:
typescriptprotected constructor() {
    super();
    
    // Check if a template already exists on the prototype
    const existingTemplate = this.constructor[$template];
    if (existingTemplate && existingTemplate !== this) {
        return existingTemplate;
    }
    
    // This is the first instance, it becomes the template
    this.constructor[$template] = this;
}
This way:

First new $UserAtom() creates an instance, stores it as the template
Subsequent new $UserAtom() returns that same template instance
All instances are literally the same object

The tricky part is Chemistry expects each instance to be separate. If they're all the same object, parent relationships and other instance-specific data could get confused. You might need to track that Atoms behave differently in the framework.RetryDHere's some gorgeous code to bend your mind. Try to explain to both of us what I am accomplishing here and why!?!const $formed = Symbol("$Arom.formed");
const $formation = Symbol("$Arom.formation");export class $Atom extends $Chemical {    /** @internal */
    [$formed] = false;    /** @internal */
    [$formation]!: Promise<void>;    protected get formed() { return this[$formed]; }
    async formation() {
        if (!this[$formed])
            await this[$formation];
    }    protected constructor() {
        super();
        const type = this.__getType() as any as typeof $Atom;
        if (this === this[$template]) {
            type._particle = this;
            this[$formation] = this.reform().then(async (formed) => {
                this[$formed] = formed;
                if (!this[$formed])
                    await this.form();
                    await this.remember();
                    this[$formed] = true;
            })
        }
        return type._particle;
    }    protected async form() { }
    protected async reform(): Promise<boolean> { return true; }
    protected async remember(): Promise<void> { }    private static _particle: $Atom = undefined as any;
    static particle<T extends $Atom = $Atom>(): T {
        if (!this._particle) 
            this._particle = new this();
        return this._particle as any;
    }}2 / 2This is a sophisticated singleton pattern with async initialization guarantees! Let me trace through what's happening:
The Singleton Enforcement:
When new $Atom() is called, the constructor:

Checks if this === this[$template] (only true for the first instance)
If it's the first instance, stores it as type._particle
Returns type._particle instead of this

So every new $Atom() returns the same instance - the first one created becomes the permanent singleton.
The Async Formation Pattern:
The brilliant part is the formation tracking:

$formed - boolean flag indicating if initialization is complete
$formation - Promise that resolves when initialization is done

When the singleton is created (first instance):

Calls reform() to attempt loading persisted state (returns boolean)
If reform() returns false, calls form() for first-time setup
Calls remember() to persist the state
Sets $formed = true when complete

The formation() Gateway:
typescriptasync formation() {
    if (!this[$formed])
        await this[$formation];
}
This is the key - any code can await formation() to ensure the Atom is fully initialized before using it. If it's already formed, it returns immediately. If not, it waits for the initialization Promise.
Why This Design:

The constructor can't be async, but you need async initialization
Multiple callers might request the singleton before it's ready
Everyone waits on the same Promise, avoiding duplicate initialization
The formed flag prevents re-initialization

The particle() static method provides a clean accessor, but the constructor enforcement means even new $UserAtom() returns the singleton. This ensures Atoms are true singletons with guaranteed async initialization, perfect for persisted global state.RetryDAnd I am referencing the type right in the constructor and this won't case any bizarre loops?No, this won't cause loops! The constructor references the type (the class constructor function) through this.__getType(), but you're only accessing static properties on it, not creating new instances of it.
The flow is linear:

new $UserAtom() called
Constructor runs, gets type = $UserAtom (the constructor function)
First time: Sets type._particle = this (stores instance on the class)
Returns type._particle (the stored instance)

The key insight is that you're storing the singleton instance as a static property on the class itself (type._particle), not creating new instances. When the constructor returns type._particle, it's returning an already-created instance, not calling new again.
The only recursion risk would be if form(), reform(), or remember() tried to create new instances of the same Atom type, but they don't. They're just initialization methods.
This pattern is actually clever because:

The class holds its own singleton instance
The constructor enforces singleton by returning the stored instance
No loops because you never call new during construction
Each Atom subclass gets its own _particle static property

The type reference in the constructor is safe - you're using the class as a storage location, not instantiating it.RetryDSo what happens to future atoms? Polymorphic singleton...ismThis isn't a standard named pattern in TypeScript or Gang of Four patterns. You've created something unique by combining:

Singleton pattern - One instance per class
Template Method pattern - The formation/reform/remember hooks
Prototype pattern - Using JavaScript's prototype chain for storage
Factory pattern - The constructor acting as a factory returning existing instances

The closest traditional pattern might be "Multiton" (a variation of Singleton where you have a fixed number of instances keyed by some identifier), but yours is different because:

The key is the class itself
It works through inheritance automatically
Each subclass gets exactly one instance without explicit registration

In other languages:

Java/C# would need explicit static fields on each subclass or a factory with a map
Python might use metaclasses to achieve something similar
Ruby could do this with class instance variables

Your pattern leverages JavaScript/TypeScript specific features:

Constructor functions as objects that can hold properties
Ability to return different objects from constructors
Prototype chain for template storage

You could call it "Class-Scoped Singleton" or "Hierarchical Singleton" - where singleton behavior is inherited but instances are scoped to their defining class. It's a legitimate pattern that emerges from Chemistry's architecture, solving the problem of global state that needs to be singleton per type rather than globally shared.
The async formation aspect adds another layer - these aren't just singletons, they're "Lazy Initialized Async Singletons" with persistence hooks. That's definitely not in any pattern book I know of.RetryDRight but that is so fundamental that this could be a language feature:singleton class SingletonFamily {

The typeof operator in JavaScript returns strings like "object", "string", "number", etc. To get the actual class constructor, you need this.constructor, which returns the function that created the instance.

typescriptprivate _createBonds(chain?: any[]) {
    if (!chain) chain = this._getDescendancyChain();
    const properties = this._findProperties(chain);
    
    for (const [property, descriptor] of properties) {
        if (this._bonds.has(property)) continue;
        
        // Check if template already has this bond
        const templateBond = this._chemical.__template?.[$formula]?.bonds?.get(property);
        if (templateBond && this._chemical !== this._chemical.__template) {
            // Reuse template's bond internals but with this instance
            const bond = new $Bond(this._chemical, property, templateBond.descriptor);
            bond._action = templateBond._action?.bind(this._chemical);
            // Copy other needed properties
        } else {

        }

        bond.init();
    }

typescriptinit() {
    const property = this._property;

    // Check if template already has this bond initialized

                },
                writable: true,
                enumerable: true,
                configurable: true,

                descriptor.get?.bind(this._chemical) : undefined;

    const descriptor = this._descriptor;

    const templateBond = this._chemical.__template?.[$formula]?.bonds?.get(property);
    
    if (templateBond && this._chemical !== this._chemical.__template) {
        // Instance inheriting from template - reuse bond configuration but with this instance
        this._isMethod = templateBond._isMethod;

            // Reuse the action but bind to this instance
            this._action = templateBond._action ? 
                descriptor.value.bind(this._chemical) : 
                descriptor.value.bind(this._chemical);

                value: (...args: any[]) => {
                    return this.bondCall(this._chemical, ...args);
                },
                writable: true,
                enumerable: true,
                configurable: true,
            };
        } else {

                this._getter = descriptor.get?.bind(this._chemical);
                this._setter = descriptor.set?.bind(this._chemical);

                enumerable: true,

            this._setter = descriptor.set?.bind(this._chemical);
            this._backingField = descriptor.value;

                enumerable: true,
                configurable: true,
            };

typescriptinit() {
    const property = this._property;
    const descriptor = this._descriptor;

        this._setter = descriptor.set?.bind(this._chemical);

            enumerable: true,
            configurable: true,
        };

When you do Object.create(this.$template), the template becomes the prototype of the new object. So the instance inherits all the template's properties through the prototype chain.
When the template's init() does:
typescriptif (this._chemical.__isTemplate)
    Object.defineProperty(this._chemical, property, this._propertyDescriptor);
It defines properties directly on the template object. When you create an instance via Object.create(template), those properties are accessible on the instance through prototype inheritance.
So when you call instance.$increment(), JavaScript looks for $increment:

First on the instance itself - not there (because the bond only defined it on template)
Then on the prototype (template) - found it!
Executes the template's $increment which uses the template's bond

That's why the template's formula is being used - because the method is actually the template's method being called through prototype inheritance.
The fix is to have EVERY chemical (template AND instances) define properties on themselves:
typescript// Remove the condition - always define on the chemical
Object.defineProperty(this._chemical, property, this._propertyDescriptor);
This way instances override the inherited template properties with their own versions that use their own bonds and formulas.RetryDI don't see the condition:

They can be shadowed but they don't redirect to the prototype?You're right to question this. Fields (regular properties) ARE inherited from the prototype in JavaScript. When you access a property on an object, JavaScript looks:

First on the object itself
Then on its prototype chain

So when you do:
typescriptconst chemical = Object.create(this.$template);
If the template has properties like count = 0, the instance can access them through the prototype chain. But symbol properties are different - they're not automatically visible through prototype inheritance unless explicitly accessed.
The issue with __template:
typescriptget __template(): this {
    return this[$type][$template] as any;
}
You're setting chemical[$type] = this.$template[$type] in createChemical, which should make both the instance and template point to the same type/constructor. So __template should work correctly.
But here's the critical question: When bonds are created and init() is called, which properties are they looking at? The bond's _descriptor comes from where?
In _findProperties(), you're walking the prototype chain and getting property descriptors. If a property exists on the template (like a method that was already wrapped), Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor on the instance won't see it - it only looks at own properties, not inherited ones.
So the instance's bonds might not be finding the template's already-wrapped methods, and might not be overriding them. The instance ends up calling the template's wrapped method through inheritance, which uses the template's bond and formula.
The fix might be to use Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor on each level of the prototype chain, or ensure instances always override inherited wrapped methods.RetryDBut that's why you are checking the formula of the template. typescriptinit() {
    const property = this._property;
    const descriptor = this._descriptor;
    
    // Check if template already has this bond initialized
    const templateBond = this._chemical.__template?.[$formula]?.bonds?.get(property);
    
    if (templateBond && !this._chemical.__isTemplate) {
        // Copy configuration from template bond
        this._isMethod = templateBond._isMethod;
        this._action = templateBond._action;
        this._getter = templateBond._getter;
        this._setter = templateBond._setter;
        this._backingField = templateBond._backingField;
        
        // Rebind everything to this instance
        if (this._action) {
            this._action = this._action.bind(this._chemical);
        }
        if (this._getter) {
            this._getter = this._getter.bind(this._chemical);
        }
        if (this._setter) {
            this._setter = this._setter.bind(this._chemical);
        }
    } else {
        // Original initialization
        this._isMethod = typeof descriptor.value === 'function' && !descriptor.get && !descriptor.set;
        
        if (this._isMethod) {
            this._action = descriptor.value.bind(this._chemical);
        } else {
            this._getter = descriptor.get?.bind(this._chemical);
            this._setter = descriptor.set?.bind(this._chemical);
            this._backingField = descriptor.value;
        }
    }
    
    // Create property descriptor - same for both paths
    if (this._isMethod) {
        this._propertyDescriptor = {
            value: (...args: any[]) => {
                return this.bondCall(this._chemical, ...args);
            },
            writable: true,
            enumerable: true,
            configurable: true,
        };
    } else {
        this._propertyDescriptor = {
            get: () => this.bondGet(),
            set: (value: any) => this.bondSet(value),
            enumerable: true,
            configurable: true,
        };
    }
    
    // Always define property to override inherited ones
    Object.defineProperty(this._chemical, property, this._propertyDescriptor);
}
This is cleaner - copy what exists from the template bond, rebind to current instance, then create the property descriptor the same way regardless.RetryDShouldn't it be done here??

typescriptconstructor(chemical: T, property: string, descriptor: PropertyDescriptor) {
    this._chemical = chemical;
    this._property = property;
    this._isProp = isProp(property, descriptor);
    
    // Check if template has this bond already
    const templateBond = chemical.__template?.[$formula]?.bonds?.get(property);

        // Copy from template bond
        this._descriptor = templateBond._descriptor;
        this._isMethod = templateBond._isMethod;
        this._action = templateBond._action;
        this._getter = templateBond._getter;
        this._setter = templateBond._setter;

            this._action = originalAction.bind(this._chemical);

            this._getter = originalGetter.bind(this._chemical);
        }

            const originalSetter = this._setter;
            this._setter = originalSetter.bind(this._chemical);
        }

        this._descriptor = descriptor;
    }
}

typescriptconstructor(chemical: T, property: string, descriptor: PropertyDescriptor) {
    this._chemical = chemical;
    this._property = property;
    this._isProp = isProp(property, descriptor);

    const templateBond = chemical.__template?.[$formula]?.bonds?.get(property);

        // Just copy everything from template bond and rebind
        Object.assign(this, templateBond);

        this._chemical = chemical;
        if (this._action) {
            this._action = this._action.bind(chemical);

            this._getter = this._getter.bind(chemical);
        }
        if (this._setter) {

This arrow function closes over this (the bond). When the method is called, it uses the bond's _chemical to call bondCall.
The issue is that the template's property descriptor is inherited by the instance. When you call the method on the instance, it's calling the template's property descriptor, which has a closure over the template's bond, which uses the template's formula.
The minimal fix in init() would be:
typescriptinit() {
    const property = this._property;
    const descriptor = this._descriptor;
    
    // Just always create the property descriptor fresh for this bond
    this._isMethod = typeof descriptor.value === 'function' && !descriptor.get && !descriptor.set;
    
    if (this._isMethod) {
        this._action = descriptor.value.bind(this._chemical);
        this._propertyDescriptor = {
            value: (...args: any[]) => {
                return this.bondCall(this._chemical, ...args);
            },
            writable: true,
            enumerable: true,
            configurable: true,
        };
    } else {
        this._getter = descriptor.get?.bind(this._chemical);
        this._setter = descriptor.set?.bind(this._chemical);
        this._backingField = descriptor.value;
        this._propertyDescriptor = {
            get: () => this.bondGet(),
            set: (value: any) => this.bondSet(value),
            enumerable: true,
            configurable: true,
        };
    }
    
    // Always define property to override inherited ones
    Object.defineProperty(this._chemical, property, this._propertyDescriptor);
}

    get state() { return $RepastedDFor all the fields and gettable properties, I would like this to return an object with each of the property names and values:Use the backingField or underlying getter, rather than bond call. Use $Represent.symbolizeSo you are implementing the diagram getterLooking at your code, you want the diagram getter to return an object with all the reactive properties and their current values. Here's the implementation:
typescriptget diagram() {
    const result: Record<string, any> = {};
    
    for (const [property, bond] of this._bonds) {
        // Skip methods and private/special properties
        if (bond._isMethod) continue;
        if (property.startsWith('_')) continue;
        if (property.startsWith('$') && property !== '$key') continue;
        
        // Get the raw value without triggering reactivity
        let value;
        if (bond._getter) {
            // Temporarily disable reactivity to avoid side effects
            const wasReactive = this._chemical[$reactive];
            this._chemical[$reactive] = false;
            value = bond._getter();
            this._chemical[$reactive] = wasReactive;
        } else {
            value = bond._backingField;
        }
        
        // Symbolize the value for serialization
        result[property] = $Represent.symbolize(value);
    }
    
    return result;
}
This implementation:

Iterates through all bonds in the formula
Skips methods (only includes properties)
Skips private properties (starting with _) and most $ properties
Gets the raw value using the getter or backing field
Temporarily disables reactivity while getting values to avoid side effects
Uses $Represent.symbolize to serialize the values
Returns an object with property names as keys and symbolized values

This gives you a snapshot of the chemical's current state without triggering any reactive updates or bond formations.RetryDNo way wrong. First, just store all the properties. typescriptget diagram() {

        // Skip methods

            value = bond._backingField;
        }
        
        // Symbolize and store
        result[property] = $Represent.symbolize(value);
    }

}

    static literalize<T = any>(symbolization: string):pastedDOkay new we need to write a Formula method called read. I tweaked the diagram to return just a string. You will need to do a json.parse, and then you will need the $Represent.literalize function. When a bond is there, update the setter - if there is one - or backing field. If it's a get only property, ignore it. If there is no bond, add a new bond for itHere's the read method for Formula:
typescriptread(diagram: string) {
    const data = JSON.parse(diagram);
    const literalized = $Represent.literalize(data);
    
    if (!literalized || typeof literalized !== 'object') return;
    
    for (const [property, value] of Object.entries(literalized)) {
        const bond = this._bonds.get(property);
        
        if (bond) {
            // Update existing bond
            if (bond.setter) {
                bond.setter(value);
            } else if (!bond.getter) {
                // Only update backing field if it's not a getter-only property
                bond.backingField = value;
            }
            // Skip getter-only properties
        } else {
            // Create new bond for unknown property
            const descriptor = {
                value: value,
                writable: true,
                enumerable: true,
                configurable: true
            };
            const newBond = new $Bond(this._chemical, property, descriptor);
            this._bonds.set(property, newBond);
            newBond.init();
        }
    }
}
This method:

Parses the JSON diagram string
Literalizes it to restore object references and Chemical instances
For each property in the data:

If a bond exists: updates via setter or backing field (skips getter-only)
If no bond exists: creates a new bond with that value


New bonds are initialized to define the property on the chemical
RetryDNow we have to write a default implementation of reform and remember. Which web tech makes the most sense to integrate with by default? export class $Atom extends $Chemical {    /** @internal */
    [$formed] = false;    /** @internal */
    [$formation]!: Promise<void>;    protected get formed() { return this[$formed]; }
    async formation() {
        if (!this[$formed])
            await this[$formation];
    }    /** @internal */
    [$remembered] = false;
    get remembered() { return this[$remembered]; }    protected constructor() {
        super();
        if (this.__isTemplate) {
            this[$formation] = this.reform().then(async (remembered) => {
                this[$formed] = remembered;
                this[$remembered] = remembered;
                if (!this[$formed])
                    await this.form();
                    await this.remember();
                    this[$formula].init();
                    this[$formed] = true;
            })
        }
        return this.__template;
    }    protected async form() { }
    protected async reform(): Promise<boolean> { return true; }
    protected async remember(): Promise<void> { }    static particle<T extends $Atom = $Atom>(): T {

        return this[$template] as any;
    }}For default reform and remember implementations, localStorage makes the most sense because:

It's synchronous (but we wrap it async for consistency)
Works everywhere (browser, mobile webview, Electron)
Persists across page refreshes/navigation
Simple API
5-10MB capacity is plenty for most atoms

Here's the implementation:
typescriptprotected async reform(): Promise<boolean> {
    try {
        const key = `chemistry.atom.${this[$type].name}`;
        const stored = localStorage.getItem(key);
        if (stored) {
            const diagram = JSON.parse(stored);
            this[$formula].read(diagram);
            return true;
        }
    } catch (e) {
        console.error(`Failed to reform ${this[$type].name}:`, e);
    }
    return false;
}

protected async remember(): Promise<void> {
    try {
        const key = `chemistry.atom.${this[$type].name}`;
        const diagram = this[$formula].diagram;
        localStorage.setItem(key, diagram);
    } catch (e) {
        console.error(`Failed to remember ${this[$type].name}:`, e);
    }
}
This default implementation:

Uses the class name as the storage key (unique per Atom type)
Stores/retrieves the formula's diagram (all reactive properties)
Handles errors gracefully (storage might be full/disabled)
Works immediately without configuration

Developers can override these methods to use:

IndexedDB for larger data
SessionStorage for tab-specific persistence
Network storage for cloud sync

private bondForm(value: $Chemical) {
        value[$formula].track();

        value[$lastState] = value[$state];
        value[$state] = this._chemical[$state];

typescriptclass $Formula {

For the recursive checking with nested state access, you could pass a context object through the check phase:
typescriptclass CheckContext {
    private accessed: Map<$Chemical, Set<string>> = new Map();
    
    markAccessed(chemical: $Chemical, property: string) {
        if (!this.accessed.has(chemical)) {
            this.accessed.set(chemical, new Set());
        }
        this.accessed.get(chemical)!.add(property);
    }
    
    recheck() {
        for (const [chemical, properties] of this.accessed) {
            const $chem$ = chemical as any;
            for (const prop of properties) {
                $chem$[prop]; // Re-access to capture mutations
            }
        }
    }
}
This would let you track exactly what was accessed during an async operation and recheck those specific paths when the promise resolves.RetryDIs it enough to know that there's a queue of promises in useEffect? If I could somehow know that there are outstanding promises for the component, could I put a then on the completion of them all that somehow checks for state changes and triggers an update if it finds any?Yes, that's a cleaner approach! You could track outstanding promises in the formula and batch check/update when they all resolve. Here's how:
typescriptclass $Formula {
    private _pendingPromises = new Set<Promise<any>>();
    
    addPendingPromise(promise: Promise<any>) {
        this._pendingPromises.add(promise);
        
        promise.finally(() => {
            this._pendingPromises.delete(promise);
            
            // If this was the last pending promise
            if (this._pendingPromises.size === 0) {
                // Re-check all accessed properties
                this.checkAsync();
            }
        });
    }
    
    private checkAsync() {
        // Only update if component is mounted (has _setState)
        if (!this._setState) return;
        
        // Re-access properties that were accessed during async operations
        this.check();
        
        // Compare state and trigger update if changed
        const currentState = this.state;
        if (currentState !== this._lastAsyncState) {
            this.update();
        }
        this._lastAsyncState = currentState;
    }
}
Then in bondCall:
typescriptprivate bondCall(chemical: $Chemical, ...args: any[]): any {
    const formula = chemical[$formula];
    formula.bindState();
    formula.track();
    let result = this.action!(...args);
    
    if (result instanceof Promise) {
        formula.addPendingPromise(result);
        // Don't update state immediately for async
        formula.unbind();
        return result;
    }
    
    // Sync path remains the same
    formula.check();
    formula.updateState();
    formula.unbind();
    return result;
}
This way:

All promises from a method are tracked
When the last one completes, you re-check everything that was accessed
Only trigger one update even if multiple async operations complete around the same time
The component only updates if it's still mounted (has _setState)

For the async state tracking, you could do something like:
typescriptclass $Formula {
    private _pendingPromises = new Set<Promise<any>>();
    private _asyncCheckContext?: CheckContext;  // Track what was accessed during async
    
    addPendingPromise(promise: Promise<any>, checkContext: CheckContext) {
        this._pendingPromises.add(promise);
        
        // Merge this promise's check context with any existing
        if (!this._asyncCheckContext) {
            this._asyncCheckContext = checkContext;
        } else {
            // Merge accessed properties from multiple async operations
            this._asyncCheckContext.merge(checkContext);
        }
        
        promise.finally(() => {
            this._pendingPromises.delete(promise);
            
            if (this._pendingPromises.size === 0 && this._asyncCheckContext) {
                // All promises done - recheck everything that was accessed
                this._asyncCheckContext.recheck();
                this._asyncCheckContext = undefined;
                
                // Only update if bound and mounted
                if (this._setState && this._chemical.__isBound) {
                    this.updateState();
                }
            }
        });
    }
}
The __isBound check is smart - unbound components share the template's component, so they wouldn't have the right _setState anyway. This ensures only the actual mounted, bound instance tries to update React state.RetryDBut how do we tell the bond to somehow know that a change was related to the async method? To the promise that was returned?The bond needs to track which properties were accessed during the async method execution and only recheck those specific properties when the promise resolves. You could pass a tracking context through the async execution:
typescriptprivate bondCall(chemical: $Chemical, ...args: any[]): any {
    const formula = chemical[$formula];
    formula.bindState();
    
    // Create a tracking context for this specific method call
    const trackingContext = new TrackingContext();
    formula.track(trackingContext);  // Pass context to track
    
    let result = this.action!(...args);
    
    if (result instanceof Promise) {
        // Pass the context with what was accessed during this method
        formula.addPendingPromise(result, trackingContext);
        formula.unbind();
        return result;
    }
    
    // Sync path - check with the same context
    formula.check(trackingContext);
    formula.updateState();
    formula.unbind();
    return result;
}
Then in the formula:
typescripttrack(context?: TrackingContext) {
    this._bonds.forEach(bond => {
        bond.accessed = false;
        bond.trackingContext = context;  // Associate with this specific call
    });
}

// In bondGet
private bondGet() {
    this.accessed = true;
    if (this.trackingContext) {
        // Record this access in the specific context
        this.trackingContext.recordAccess(this._chemical, this._property);
    }
    // ... rest of bondGet
}
Now when the promise resolves, you only recheck the properties that were accessed during that specific async method call, not everything. Each async method tracks its own set of accessed properties.RetryDBut an async method call spans many other method calls. It could take ten minutesYou're right - an async method could trigger other async operations, make network calls, etc. over a long period. The tracking needs to capture the entire async execution chain, not just the initial synchronous portion.
The challenge is that after the initial synchronous execution of the async method, any subsequent code runs outside the tracking context. When the async method resumes after an await, it doesn't know it should be tracking property access anymore.
One approach could be to use async context tracking (like Node's AsyncLocalStorage concept):
typescriptclass $Formula {
    private _activeTrackingContexts = new Map<Promise<any>, TrackingContext>();
    
    async wrapAsync<T>(promise: Promise<T>, context: TrackingContext): Promise<T> {
        this._activeTrackingContexts.set(promise, context);
        try {
            // Somehow make this context available during async execution
            const result = await promise;
            return result;
        } finally {
            this._activeTrackingContexts.delete(promise);
        }
    }
}
But JavaScript doesn't have built-in async context propagation. You'd need to somehow maintain the tracking context across await boundaries.
Alternatively, you might need to make tracking explicit for async methods:
typescriptasync $fetchData() {
    const context = this[$formula].startAsyncTracking();
    
    const data = await fetch('/api/data');
    this.data = await data.json();  // This access gets tracked
    
    await this.$processData();  // This method's accesses also tracked
    
    context.complete();  // Triggers recheck of everything accessed
}
Or accept that async methods track only their initial synchronous execution, and subsequent state changes trigger their own updates through the normal reactivity system. That might be simpler and more predictable.RetryDWell wait, that wrapper will work for me to do something BEFORE the promise executes right, because it's guaranteed to be before the promise runs.     };
}, [chemical]);
Then in the promise resolution, compare against that render state to detect unrecorded changes.
Deduping Async Calls in Constructor: You could track active async operations by method signature:
typescriptclass $Formula {
    private _activeAsyncOps = new Map<string, Promise<any>>();
    
    trackAsyncOperation(methodName: string, promise: Promise<any>) {
        // If same method already running, return existing promise
        if (this._activeAsyncOps.has(methodName)) {
            return this._activeAsyncOps.get(methodName);
        }
        
        this._activeAsyncOps.set(methodName, promise);
        
        promise.finally(() => {
            this._activeAsyncOps.delete(methodName);
            // Check if state changed since last render
            if (this._renderState !== this.state) {
                this.update();
            }
        });
        
        return promise;
    }
}
The risk with deduping is that the method might have side effects beyond state updates. But for idempotent data fetching, it could prevent unnecessary repeated calls during re-renders triggered by the async operation itself.
The challenge is that without true async context tracking in JavaScript, you can't automatically track all property access throughout an async operation's entire lifetime. You might need to accept that limitation and rely on explicit state updates triggering their own re-renders.RetryDTrue. But you agree - it is a useful feature to have the concept of making an async call in the bond constructor (synchronously) and being smart enough to say that if the rerender was caused by that call, to then not actually perform the next call - silently swallowing itIt is a sophisticated abstraction that does insist upon idempotency - BUT if you are doing something non-idempotent in the bond constructor, which happens before a render, you have probably caused an infinite loop! So this assumption is relatively safe, and the user experiences it as a change that didn't happen, rather than a render stack overflowYou're right - this is a useful abstraction for preventing infinite loops in async bond constructors. The pattern makes sense:

Async method called in bond constructor during render
Method completes, triggers state update, causes re-render
Same bond constructor runs again
Chemistry detects "this async call triggered the current render" and silently skips it

The implementation could track causality:
typescriptclass $Formula {
    private _renderTrigger?: string; // What caused this render
    private _activeAsyncOps = new Map<string, { promise: Promise<any>, id: string }>();
    
    trackAsyncOperation(methodName: string, promise: Promise<any>): Promise<any> {
        const opId = `${methodName}-${Date.now()}`;
        
        // If this render was triggered by this method, skip
        if (this._renderTrigger === methodName) {
            console.log(`Skipping ${methodName} - it triggered this render`);
            return Promise.resolve(); // Silent no-op
        }
        
        this._activeAsyncOps.set(methodName, { promise, id: opId });
        
        promise.finally(() => {
            this._activeAsyncOps.delete(methodName);
            
            // Mark that this method triggered the next render
            this._renderTrigger = methodName;
            this.update();
            
            // Clear trigger after update
            setTimeout(() => this._renderTrigger = undefined, 0);
        });
        
        return promise;
    }
}
You're correct that non-idempotent operations in bond constructors are already dangerous - they'd likely cause infinite loops anyway. This abstraction turns potential stack overflows into silent no-ops, which is much better developer experience.
The assumption of idempotency is reasonable for constructor operations - they should be setting up state based on children, not performing side effects.RetryDNon idempotent operations are dangerous period in UI! Might it be the meaning of a $method that it is idempotent? If it is not, the user is somehow asking that method to act like a for loop at best, triggering a finite set of renders, otherwise it is an infinite loop! So in the docs, we say "When marking a property or method with $, you are agreeing to idempotency from the perspective of the UI, and we use that assumption to enable ease of useThat's a powerful conceptual framework! The $ prefix becomes a contract: "This method/property is idempotent and safe for UI reactivity." This gives Chemistry semantic meaning beyond just "this is reactive."
The documentation could frame it as:
The $ Contract: When you prefix a method with $, you're declaring it's idempotent - calling it multiple times with the same state produces the same result. Chemistry uses this guarantee to:

Safely dedupe repeated calls during re-renders
Optimize reactivity by assuming predictable behavior
Prevent infinite loops by skipping redundant operations

For example:
typescriptclass $TodoList extends $Chemical {
    // Safe - idempotent, can be called repeatedly
    $loadTodos() {
        this.todos = fetchTodos();  // Same result each time
    }
    
    // Unsafe - would violate the $ contract
    $incrementCounter() {
        this.counter++;  // NOT idempotent! Each call changes state
    }
}
The second example would cause infinite re-renders, violating the contract. Chemistry could even warn in development when it detects non-idempotent patterns.
This turns a potential footgun (async operations in constructors) into a feature with clear semantics. Users understand that $ methods are "UI-safe" operations that Chemistry can optimize and dedupe as needed.
The idempotency assumption also justifies other optimizations - Chemistry could batch multiple $ method calls, reorder them, or cache results, knowing the contract guarantees consistent outcomes.RetryDAh okay, well then it's tricky, because $increment is fine as a handler. So that an async $method called in the bond constructor is assumed to be idempotent. It's a complicated line of code, but my guess is that someone is doing something weird if they called an async method that they expected to possibly cause a state change that - upon rerender - might or might not cause another, while definitely knowing that the chain of might or might not side effects is finiteYou're right - the context matters. $increment as a click handler is perfectly fine because it's triggered by user interaction, not by rendering. The idempotency requirement only applies to async methods called during the bond constructor (during render).
The distinction is:

Handler methods ($onClick, etc.): Can be non-idempotent, triggered by events
Async methods in bond constructor: Should be idempotent to prevent render loops

Anyone calling an async method in a bond constructor that intentionally creates a finite chain of state changes is doing something unusual. That pattern suggests they're trying to use rendering as a control flow mechanism, which is problematic. The framework assumption that "async methods called during render should be idempotent" is reasonable.
The code could detect this:
typescriptprivate bondCall(chemical: $Chemical, ...args: any[]): any {
    const formula = chemical[$formula];
    const inConstructor = formula.inBondConstructor; // Set during bond constructor execution
    
    formula.bindState();
    formula.track();
    let result = this.action!(...args);
    
    if (result instanceof Promise && inConstructor) {
        // This async method was called during bond constructor
        result = formula.dedupeAsyncConstructorCall(this._property, result);
    }
    
    // ... rest of bondCall
}
This way Chemistry can be smart about deduping async calls during render while leaving event handlers unrestricted. The framework makes the right assumptions for the common case while preventing the most likely bugs.RetryDAh! a very very simple silly and powerful method will be enabled by this framework!async increment() {
     this.renderCount++;
}$Class() {
    this.increment()
}If that is called synchronously in the bond constructor, and we do this right, that should reflect the number of "causal" renders, ignoring its self-effect. renderCount is a weird number, but it is meaningful somehow, though I am not sure whyThat's a subtle but meaningful metric! The renderCount would track "external causality" - how many times the component re-rendered due to outside state changes (parent re-renders, prop changes, other components' state updates) but NOT due to its own async state updates.
typescriptclass $Component extends $Chemical {
    renderCount = 0;
    
    async incrementRenderCount() {
        this.renderCount++;
    }
    
    $Component() {
        this.incrementRenderCount(); // Called on each external render
    }
}
With your deduping logic:

First render: incrementRenderCount() runs, sets renderCount = 1
When promise resolves, triggers re-render
Second render: Chemistry detects this was self-triggered, skips the call
Parent changes something, triggers third render
Third render: Not self-triggered, incrementRenderCount() runs, sets renderCount = 2

So renderCount becomes a measure of how many times external forces caused this component to render, filtering out its own feedback loops. This could be useful for:

Performance debugging (which parent is triggering excessive renders?)
Understanding component lifecycle patterns
Detecting unwanted render cascades from other parts of the app

It's like a "net render count" that excludes the component's own state management noise. The fact that this emerges naturally from your async deduping mechanism is elegant - the framework feature enables a debugging tool for free.RetryDI think what's cool is you can put that number IN the UI! typescriptclass $Book extends $Chemical {
    chapterTypeCount: Record<string, number> = {};

        this.chapterTypeCount[typeName] = (this.chapterTypeCount[typeName] || 0) + 1;

}
Each non-self-triggered render would increment these counts, giving you a live view of what types of children have been passed over time. typescriptclass $AdaptiveList extends $Chemical {
    itemTypeFrequency: Record<string, number> = {};
    
    async trackItemType(item: $ListItem) {
        const type = item.constructor.name;
        this.itemTypeFrequency[type] = (this.itemTypeFrequency[type] || 0) + 1;
    }
    
    $AdaptiveList(...items: $ListItem[]) {
        items.forEach(item => this.trackItemType(item));
    }
    
    view() {
        // Adapt UI based on usage patterns
        const mostCommonType = Object.entries(this.itemTypeFrequency)
            .sort(([,a], [,b]) => b - a)[0]?.[0];
        
        if (mostCommonType === 'ImageItem') {
            // Switch to grid layout for image-heavy content
            return <div className="grid">{this.children}</div>;
        } else {
            // Use list layout for text-heavy content
            return <div className="list">{this.children}</div>;
        }
    }
}

Moving form() to $Chemical and making it conditionally async is clean:
typescriptclass $Chemical {
    constructor() {
        // ... existing constructor logic ...
        
        // After all construction is complete
        const formation = this.form();
        if (formation instanceof Promise) {
            this[$formation] = formation.then(() => {
                this[$formed] = true;
                // Trigger single update when async initialization completes
                if (this.__isBound && this[$formula]._setState) {
                    this[$formula].updateState();
                }
            });
        } else {
            this[$formed] = true;
        }
    }
    
    protected form(): void | Promise<void> {
        // Default is sync, subclasses can override with async
    }
}
JavaScript guarantees constructor execution order - the base class constructor completes before derived class constructors run, and all constructors complete synchronously before any async code executes. So even if form() is called in the base constructor, the entire object construction happens predictably first.
This means any Chemical can opt into async initialization just by making form() async. The component will render with initial state, then update once when the formation completes. No await needed in user code, no complex state management - just override form() and return a promise.
The deduping logic you're considering would prevent re-initialization loops, making this pattern safe for any Chemical, not just Atoms. It's a powerful abstraction that hides async complexity while providing predictable behavior.RetryDNo but you know what? I wouldn't. Here's why. Then any chemical might need to be awaited. Type safety - the layout can require certain methods on pages
No prop drilling - the layout maintains its own state

The syntax this.layout.view(this) is concise and clear - "render me through my layout." The layout becomes a rendering context that pages opt into, rather than a wrapper imposed from above. This fits Chemistry's philosophy of components managing their own relationships rather than having structure imposed externally.RetryDWell it just looks like a page that takes a layout in its constructor or bond constructor, and otherwise, has a different view method, because the base class page will enforce the containment relationshipYou're right - the relationship is cleaner when enforced through Chemistry's patterns:
typescriptclass $Page extends $Chemical {
    layout = $LayoutAtom.particle();  // All pages get the singleton layout
    
    $Page(layout?: $LayoutAtom) {
        // Can override with different layout if needed
        if (layout) this.layout = layout;
    }
    
    // Pages must implement content()
    abstract content(): ReactNode;
    
    // Base page enforces the containment
    view() {
        return this.layout.view(this);
    }
}

class $HomePage extends $Page {
    content() {
        return <div>Home content</div>;
    }
    // view() is inherited - automatically renders through layout
}
The base $Page class enforces that all pages render through a layout. Each page just implements content() and gets the layout behavior for free. The layout persists as an Atom across page changes, maintaining drawer states, theme, etc.
This is cleaner than the typical React pattern where layouts wrap routes. Here, pages know about their layout and explicitly render through it, but the relationship is enforced by the base class so individual pages don't need to think about it.
The bond constructor pattern means you could even pass different layouts to different pages if needed, while defaulting to the singleton. The framework handles the complexity while the code reads naturally.
